7 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Introduction 
 
Though competency management has existed for more than 40 years, this approach to organisational development 
is just gaining credence in the public sector in Jamaica. Many developed countries , including the United States and 
the United Kingdom, widely use the development of competencies as one of the most critical elements in their public 
service development and reform. It is considered a conscious, strategic and coordinated approach to leadership 
development and training. Increasingly, in their quest to enhance the ir capacity to govern, countries have been 
employing competency frameworks as a means of developing strategic leadership capabilities in their public sector 
employees. The Government of Jamaica  (GoJ), in particular, has included this feature in its moderni sation and 
transformation processes. The GoJ, in its Cabinet Decision # 49/09 (November 2, 2009), established its position in 
rationalising the public service as a pathway to improved professionalism, effectiveness, efficiency, accountability 
and responsiveness to citizens’ needs, leading to the establishment  of a new Strategic Human Resource 
Management (SHRM) function. This function was mandated to provide strategic direction through policies and 
frameworks that support the development and management of the public service through the recruitment and 
retention of competent and motivated public officers  at all  levels capable of delivering efficient, effective and 
responsive services to the public. 
 
It is against this background that a Public Sector Learning Framework (PSLF) has been  conceived. The 
implementation of the PSLF  is being led by the Management Institute for National Development (MIND), the 
GoJ’s training, organisational and leadership development institute, with support from key strategic stakeholders, 
which include the following:  
 Office of the Cabinet 
 Ministry of Finance and the Public Service 
 Office of the Services Commission 
 Jamaica Civil Service Association  
 Planning Institute of Jamaica 
 

----- PAGE 9 -----
 
 
8 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
The PSLF is comprehensive outline that represents 
a Whole-of-Government approach needed to build 
public sector organisations capable of contributing to 
the process of sustainable national development by 
providing a mechanism to: 
 facilitate an integrated and sustainable 
approach to how learning and development are  managed across the public sector; 
 facilitate access, mobility and progression in learning and professional development paths within the 
sector; and 
 evaluate/assess investments in, and the impact of, human resource development within the sector. 
 
At the core of the Framework is the recognition 
that people are the public service’s most valuable 
asset. The commitment and capabilities of people 
underpin the ability of the public sector to deliver 
the best value public goods and services to the 
nation. The Public Sector Learning Framework 
signals the Government of Jamaica’s clear commitment to invest in human capital development . It is intended to be 
the GoJ’s blueprint for building a culture of continuous learning and innovation within the public sector. It  has eight 
(8) core elements which, when implemented, will ensure the public sector has the learning infrastructure, resources 
and expertise for responding to the challenges of driving sustainable development and building and managing a 
modern economy. The elements of the Public Sector Learning Framework are as follows: 
 
Figure 1: Elements of the Public Sector Learning Framework 
 
Immediate Outcome of the PSLF
A generic, consistent and comprehensive 
substructure that reflect the capabilities (skills, 
knowledge and abilities) which will underpin the 
Government’s ability to deliver improved results and 
provide a clear and comprehensive learning path for 
progression in the public service. 
Ultimate Outcome of the PSLF
A dynamic public service that responds to the changing needs 
of Government through a team of public service professionals 
that are competent, fluid, responsive, relevant and 
transformational in their management and leadership, such 
that they and their organisations, deliver the professionalism, 
the performance, the service and the impact that ultimately 
bring to life the National Vision for Jamaica.

9 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
The Competency Model 
 
The Government of Jamaica Competency Framework Model pulls together the Competenc y Framework and 
Professional Pathways. Its structure outlines the values, leadership attributes, core competencies, skills, knowledge, 
and behaviours required for success within each professional group within the Jamaican Public Service. This 
Framework serves as a roadmap for individuals to assess their strengths and areas for development, set goals, and 
track their progress. Additionally, the Framework assists organi sations in aligning skill sets with job roles, enabling 
effective talent management and career development. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 2: Government of Jamaica Competency Framework Model 
 
The Model outlines a comprehensive framework that encompasses key elements essential for a Whole -of-
Government leadership philosophy, reflects the core values of the public sector, and is designed to be integrated into 
the operations of Ministries, Departments, and Agencies (MDAs) across the public sector. The Framework is 
structured as follows: 
 
1. Foundational Elements: 
These serve as the bedrock of the Framework and encompass the fundamental principles that underpin a 
Whole-of-Government leadership philosophy and public sector values. These elements set the tone for the 
entire Framework. 


----- PAGE 11 -----
 
 
10 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
 
2. Core Competencies for all Levels within MDAs: 
Core Competencies play a crucial role in defining the skill sets, behaviours, and attributes expected from all 
personnel within Ministries, Departments, and Agencies (MDAs) across the public sector. They are applicable 
at all levels , underscoring their significance i n establishing a consistent and standardized foundation for 
personnel development and performance evaluation. Competency measures increase as employees 
progress through different levels. This approach acknowledges that employees should not only meet certain 
competency standards for their current roles but also continue to grow and acquire additional competencies 
as they advance in their careers within their Professional Pathway. 
 
3. Technical Competencies Aligned to Professional Pathways: 
The technical competencies are aligned with the established Government of Jamaica Professional Pathways 
and demonstrate a focused approach to career development and speciali sation within the public sector. 
These technical competencies are designed to cater to the unique demand s of different professions and 
career paths, ensuring that individuals acquire the speciali sed knowledge and skills necessary to excel in 
their chosen fields.  
 
Overall, including technical competencies aligned with Professional Pathways enhances the Frame work's 
ability to support career growth, speciali sation, and performance excellence within the public sector. It 
provides a structured and goal-oriented approach to developing the skills and expertise needed for success 
in various professions. 
 
As the publ ic sector landscape evolves, these technical competencies will be updated to reflect emerging 
trends, technologies, and best practices within specific professions. This adaptability ensures that public 
officers remain well-prepared for changing demands. 
 
4. Learning Pathways for each responding to the Technical Competencies Aligned to Professional 
Pathways: 
A Learning Pathway refers to the sequencing of learning and development interventions, as public 
officers develop the required competencies to progress alo ng a professional pathway. 1  In order to 
                                                 
1 A Learning Pathway is one of eight (8) elements of the Government of Jamaica Public Sector Learning Framework. 

----- PAGE 12 -----
 
 
11 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
meet the requirements at each professional level, learning and development options will be organised 
along three (3) streams: 
 
i. Core Competencies 
ii. Technical/Functional Competencies 
iii. Leadership Development 
 
The Learning Pathways will open the scope of learning to embrace in addition to traditional training 
products, learning experiences outside of training room settings, such as internships, apprenticeships, 
attachments, independent research and assignment to special projects. 
 
By implementing this comprehensive Framework, the public sector aims to cultivate a cohesive and capable workforce 
that upholds its values, embodies effective leadership qualities, and possesses the technical expertise necessary to 
fulfil the diverse roles within MDAs. This approach will contribute to a more efficient, responsive, and effective public 
sector that serves the needs of the government and the citizens it represents. 
  

----- PAGE 13 -----
 
 
12 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
The Competency Framework 
 
A Competency Framework defines the knowledg e, skills, and attributes that workers in an organi sation should 
possess in order to perform optimally. Each position within the organi sation will have its own set of competencies 
required for effective on -the-job performance . In the Jamaican context, the Competency Framework is one 
component of the larger Public Sector Learning Framework, and delineates the core and technical competencies 
(including knowledge, skills, and attitudes) necessary for the effective performance of public servants in the various 
Ministries, Departments and Agencies (MDAs) which comprise Jamaica’s Public Service.   
The core and technical competencies in the framework can be measured against international standards as well as 
be improved through induction, orientation , training, internship, mentoring and modelling, experience, performance 
coaching and career coaching. 
Importantly, this Framework places value on Accountability, Innovation, Integrity  and Impartiality, Inclusivity 
and Service Excellence . Public Servants are therefore ex pected to be Inspiring, Future -Oriented and 
Performance Driven in how they demonstrate competency in service delivery.  
 
An Overview of its Intended Purpose  
The GoJ Competency Framework serves to give Jamaica’s diverse public sector a shared language to d escribe the 
knowledge, skills, observable behaviours and abilities needed to perform work across all MDAs.  In addition, it defines 
a set of standards that  all public servants must attain/adhere to in order to achieve the Government’s stated strategic 
goals in the Vision 2030 Jamaica: National Development Plan and create a culture of high productivity, in which the 
delivery of quality service to every Jamaican citizen, and foreigners who may need to interface with the public sector on 
occasion, is promoted and sustained. As the new gold standard for performance in the Public Sector, it is relevant to all 
phases of the employee lifecycle; from talent acquisition to development to retention and reward.  
 
The Framework will support the government’s efforts in h uman capital development, as it aims to improve the 
processes of learning and development, recruitment and sele ction, succession planning, career planning and 
performance management.  It will be the benchmark for professional excellence, performance management and skills 
improvement. The Competency Framework provides the foundation for the design of relevant management, 
leadership and technical programmes to meet the current and future needs of public service in Jamaica.  

----- PAGE 14 -----
 
 
13 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Development of the Competency Framework included:  
 undertaking a literature review and researching international best practice s in competency  framework 
development and application; 
 documentation review and analysis of job descriptions, organisational charts, vision, mission and core 
values statements, legislative documents, the National Development Plan, the Modernisation Plan and the 
Vision 2030, among other documents;  
 establishing Technical Working Groups (TWG) (see full listing in Appendix 3) to identify job functions and 
key technical c ompetencies for core professional groups and to build on the focus group outcomes by 
drafting and refining the competencies and indicators into a consistent format; and 
 designing research questions  (see Appendix 4)  and facilitating focus groups of s takeholders across 78 
MDAs. 
The consensual, consultative and participatory approach combined with different kinds of data served to counteract 
the threats of validity identified in each, thereby accounting for a Competency Framework that is comprehensive, 
relevant, robust, and contextual. (See a detailed methodology in Appendix 2).  
  

----- PAGE 15 -----
 
 
14 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
The Professional Pathways 
 
The GoJ Professional Pathways f or the Public Service is closely aligned to the GoJ Competency Framework and 
establishes core professional groups defined by  standards of practice at each level of professional maturity. The 
Professional Pathways Guide will, among other things, seek to: 
1. elevate the level of professionalism and competency across critical technical areas in the public service through 
appropriate leadership, training and certification programmes 
2. raise the formal status of professional groups across the public service 
3. facilitate professional autonomy by: 
a. allowing for objectivity and transparency in Human Resource Management and Development decisions , 
in particular, those related to learning and development needs, promotion, recruitment, and selection 
b. serving as a source of reference and an impetus to sharpen the competencies of public sector employees 
while enhancing organisational capabilities to deal with existing and emerging challenges  
c. assisting academic institutions in aligning their curriculum to meet the industry's needs 
d. ensuring a steady supply of competent professionals in new growth areas 
e. providing a platform for self -assessment so there is  an awareness of competency gaps and for 
engagement in continuous learning to advance careers and keep abreast of new trends in the job market.  
 
Implications of the Competency Framework and Professional Pathways  for Human Resource Development 
Practices 
 
Recruitment and Selection  
The Human Resources department in the various Ministries, Departments and Agencies, along with the Office of the 
Services Commission, will use the Competency Framework at all levels of the recruitment and selection process. 
These include job analysis, application screening, shortlisting, candidate interviewing, selection and appointment of 
applicants, and induction and training phases. MDAs will also create job descriptions and design instruments for 
interviews, which include compet ency-based questions, role play, case study analysis, formal presentations, 
individual/group exercises, and other assessments for job applicants so that there is an alignment with the required  
knowledge, skills, and/or abilities needed for the particular j ob function. MDAs will  use the competency 
framework to screen applicants based on what they want the applicant to do versus what each applicant can do 
based on their resume, so it will become a critical part of candidate profiling. 
 

----- PAGE 16 -----
 
 
15 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Learning and Developmen t 
Learning refers to the acquisition of knowledge and skills through a specific developmental experience that supports 
identified performance outcomes of tasks individuals do on the job. It can be both formal and informal. Development 
refers to planned activities that enhance employees' ability to meet current and future job requirements and perform 
at higher levels. Learning and development methodologies include, but are not limited to , performance planning, 
coaching, mentoring, job shadowing, classroom-based training programmes, on-the-job training, projects, stretched 
assignments and other enrichment opportunities. The competency framework encourages the 70 -20-10 Model for 
learning and development, which posits that 70 per cent of an individual’s developm ent happens through on-the-job 
experiences, 20 per cent through engagements and feedback from others, and 10 per cent from formal training 
events2. This provides the greatest value as a general guideline for organi sations seeking to maximi se the 
effectiveness of their learning and development programmes through other activities and inputs. See related Figure 
2 below. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 3: 70-20-10 Model 
 
The Competency Framework also supports other learning and development models such as the EPIC3 (Experiential, 
People, Investigation, Course) journey which emphasizes the learners’ progression along various stages of 
competence with the ultimate destination being a targeted level of improved performance.  
 
                                                 
2 770-20-10 From Strategy to Action Retrieved on April 26, 2014 at https://www.702010forum.com//files/702010_Strategy_to_Action.pdf. This Model was developed 
by Michael Lombardo and Robert Eichinger 
3 Diane Law, Head of Learning & Development at Newton Europe, designed a tool to deliver the right training to the right people at the right time. This model, 
titled EPIC Journey, provides a practical Framework which has been adopted to guide the design of the various training programmes to be delivered under 
the L&D Strategy. 


----- PAGE 17 -----
 
 
16 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 4: EPIC Journey 
EPIC is an acronym that highlights the multi-faceted approach to learning. The components are as follows: 
 EXPERIENTIAL: This draws on on -the-job, work shadowing, new responsibilities, job swaps , special 
projects, committee memberships, secondments, volunteer work, etc. 
 PEOPLE: This includes feedback, mentorship, networks, peer groups, action learning, manager guidance, 
facilitated groups, online discussions, etc. 
 INVESTIGATION: This includes just-in-time resources, articles, videos , hints and tips, manuals, 
infographics, and bite-size learning - things you delve into when you need them 
 COURSE: including formal e-learning or face-to-face, certifications4, professional qualifications, etc. 
 
Essentially, the Competency Framework  is des igned to allow for the engagement of professionals across a 
mixture/range of developmental experiences. Therefore, learning and development interventions must reflect the 
above models to maximally develop employees in the public sector. Training and develo pment professionals 
designing interventions for any professional group will be expected to use the ir competencies to engage in the 
following: 
 assess current and emerging performance needs 
 design and develop training 
 facilitate training 
 support transfer of learning 
 evaluate training 
During the learning needs assessment phase , line managers will engage in performance conversations with direct 
reports to agree on certain competencies that may need focus  and/or improvement in order to meet expectations at 
the proficiency level. The line manager will also need to consider learning and development interventions that the 
                                                 
4 In each instance, a determination must be made about the efficacy of available national, regional or international certificat ion 


----- PAGE 18 -----
 
 
17 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
employee may need to advance to another proficiency level. The Competency Framework  will also support 
employees as they will be able to assess their own performance, identify specific opportunities to strengthen skills 
and provide quality service to customers and stakeholders. Additionally, learning and development practitioners will 
apply the competencies and indicators in the Competency Framework to develop a range of evidence -based tools, 
such as training resources and assessment instruments.  
 
Performance Management  
The performance management instruments used across the public service shall align with the Competency 
Framework. The competencies ar e qualitative in nature and will act as “contributors” to facilitate or enable 
performance. The performance management process is transparent , collaborative and evident at all levels of the 
organisation. Accordingly, line managers will be able to discuss a nd agree on the targets, goals and competencies 
that direct reports should effectively demonstrate over a period of time. Feedback on annual or quarterly performance 
should be based on set goals and competencies.  Any identified gaps between the employee’s performance and the 
proficiency levels set in the Competency Framework will highlight areas for development and should feed into the 
learning and development cycle. 
 
Succession Planning  
Succession planning is a strategic approach by which an entity motivates its employees to further their career growth 
and support its business continuity by having an adequate number of qualified, trained, and experienced employees 
ready to move horizontally or to be promoted to leadership and/or critical positions where va cancies exist. The 
Competency Framework should be used within the Human Resources Departments across the MDAs to perform a 
skills inventory directly assessing employees against the core and technical competencies, making it easier to 
determine what is already present and what is still required. Recruitment and /or training can then take place based 
on the competency gaps identified in the skills inventory. The Competency Framework  should contribute to the 
creation of job profiles for key positions to document the knowledge, skills and abilities required. 
 
The Competency Framework will aid line managers in identifying and developing the competencies employees should 
demonstrate at a particular proficiency level in the organi sation so that there is readiness fo r promotion or career 
transition. Identified gaps in performance shall be bridged through adequate learning and development activities. 
Additionally, line managers should implement employee development plans to create learning and knowledge transfer 
goals for existing and future employees. 
 

----- PAGE 19 -----
 
 
18 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
 
 
Career Development  
Career development is the lifelong process of managing learning, work, leisure, and transitions in order to move 
toward a personally determined and evolving preferred future. The Competency Framework will serve to clarify issues 
related to employability, direction, skills development, personal development and progression.  An individual looking 
to advance in public service can use the Competency Framework  to identify the necessary competencies and 
indicators required to progress, either to different roles or higher levels within the same role. It will aid the MDAs in 
developing solid career development programmes, thus attracting high-quality employees and ensuring a steady and 
continuous supply of qualified and talented workers.   
 
Through the Competency Framework, the MDAs will be able to place importance on how their employees manage 
their respective careers and will take steps that provide structure to employees’ progress on their chosen professional 
pathways. Aspiring employees to the public service will be able to examine the technical and core competency 
requirements across various professional groups and make well-considered decisions in their selection of educational 
programmes and other trainin g and development activities. The framework will help universit ies and colleges to 
develop resources for their career development programmes and initiatives , thereby helping students to transition 
smoothly from academic studies into the public service. 
 
Pr omotion  
Promotion within any professional group sh ould be informed by the Competency Framework and the professional 
pathways, ensuring that employees have consistently met or exceeded expectations in all core and technical 
competencies at a particular proficiency level. Promotion should also be informed by the results of the performance 
management and appraisal process, which shall also be aligned to the Competency Framework. Line managers are 
to engage in professional conversations with employees on future  job opportunities and the requisite performance 
levels to advance to higher positions within an MDA. 
 
Integration with other Competency Frameworks  
The Competency Framework is aligned with the strategic priorities of the GoJ, reflects current good practice , and 
shall be integrated into human resource processes and maintained to reflect a changing environment. However, an 
important consideration in applying the Competency Framework is how it should be aligned or integrated with existing 
MDA-specific frameworks in the public service. Given that the Competency Framework is comprehensive, though not 

----- PAGE 20 -----
 
 
19 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
exhaustive, the intention is that other frameworks should refer to the Competency Framework for the public service 
of Jamaica to inform any redesign or re -imagination of their existing framework. All other frameworks should follow 
its methodology, style and format to ensure synergy. Developers should also communicate with SHRMD, MIND and 
OSC for details about scope, methodology and design of any Competency Framework for any MDA. 
 
Definition of Terms 
Competencies  
Competencies are the range of integrated knowledge, skills, behaviours and personal attributes necessary to produce 
effective performance in a given task or role. 
Core Competencies  
These are the skills, behavio urs and work-related attributes required at every level in the public service of Jamaica 
but in varying degrees depending on job function. 
Technical Competencies  
Technical competencies describe the application, knowledge, and skills needed to perform effec tively in a specific  
job or group of jobs within an MDA. These types of competencies are closely aligned with the knowledge and skills 
or “know-how” needed for successful performance within a given professional group in the public service of Jamaica. 
Profession 
A profession is an occupational field that requires formal qualification and training and has defined standards that 
guide the behaviours of the practitioners in that field. 
 
Professional Development  
This is a process that requires all employees to continually update/upgrade their skills and knowledge in order to 
remain professionally competent. Each calendar year, all public sector professionals will be required to go online 
through the designated ICT platform and log a minimum of 30 hours of develo pmental activity, which should include 
at least eight hours of formal or structured learning. Given that a wide range of activities can qualify as professional 
development, the activities undertaken must be aligned with the learning pathways and approved b y the Head of 
Profession (HoP).   
 
 
 

----- PAGE 21 -----
 
 
20 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Competency Framework 
According to the ISO Competency Framework for Standards Development Professionals  (2023), a Competency 
Framework serves as a comprehensive guide that delineates the Essential, skills, and attributes  required by 
employees within an organization to achieve optimal performance. It functions as a technical or core competency 
outline, specifying competency standards and articulating the expected levels of knowledge, skills, and attitudes that 
employees sh ould possess. . It refers to a comprehensive collection of competencies and behavioural indicators 
related to performance in a specific task  with a specific level of responsibility. A Competency Framework shows the 
kind of behaviours valued and required by the organisation to achieve its objectives. Competency Frameworks are, 
therefore, linked to an organisation’s values and objectives. This Competency Framework is linked to the values of 
the public service and the strategic priorities of the Government and will serve to, among other things: 
 establish and communicate expectations for the specific behaviours that contribute to successful job 
performance 
 encourage employees to be accountable for their professional development 
 identify strategic, targeted and realistic development objectives 
 provide a baseline and open the door for performance conversations 
 promote an understanding of the competencies required to move into a new role or job. 
 
Critical Success Factors of the Competency Framework 
The Competency Framework was designed 
to ensure the following: 
 
 Scalability - can be used to assess capabilities at any level of 
an MDA. 
 Adaptability - flexible to changing needs or demands in the 
Government, can be updated/refreshed to reflect new trends 
in technical and core areas. 
 Functionality - can diagnose areas for improvement. 
 Usability - is easy to use as it explicitly outlines knowledge, 
skills, and behavioural indicators at varying levels/stages of 
professional maturity. 
 
 
  

----- PAGE 22 -----
 
 
21 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Framework Design: Core Competencies 
The primary component of the framework is an integrated set of core values that form the philosophical base of the 
framework. These values were identified and validated by the stakeholders and will support the vision, shape the 
culture and reflect what the public service of Jamaica values. They are the essence of the public service’s identity, 
practices, principles and beliefs.  
The values are then supported by three leadership clusters which include leadership-related capabilities identified by 
public servan ts in Jamaica and validated by the literature on effective leadership , such as those highlighted by 
Cooperrider & Whitney, 2005; Covey, 2004; Drath, 1998; Greenfield, 2007; Headington, 2001; Hutton, 2015; 2001; 
Ingleton, 2013; Kouzes & Posner, 2002; Maxwel l, 1998, Newman, 2005; Northhouse, 2007; Nanus, 1992; Smith, 
2014. These are the Inspiring Cluster, the Future-Oriented Cluster and the Performance Cluster.  
These clusters comprise three competencies each, amounting to nine core leadership competencies th at public 
servants are expected to demonstrate to be effective in their roles. As represented in Figure 4, the Framework 
consists of: 
 Five core values of the Public Service 
 Three leadership clusters 
 Nine core competencies mapped across the three leadership clusters 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 5: Core Competency Framework for the Public Service

Leadership Statement 
The Competency Framework does not limit leadership to a position of authority but encompasses the critically 
important civic work  performed by every public servant engaged in making a positive difference in Jamaica. It is 
guided by the philosophy that all public officers  are leaders, regardless of job function, expertise, qualification, age, 
gender, status or any other orientation.. This is a notable shift from an individualistic, static and hierarchical concept 
of leadership in the Public Service to one which engages everyone in the leadership process. That is, this Competency 
Framework has positioned all public officers as leaders whose capacities can be developed through leadership 
education and leadership development. The leadership statement is intended to evoke a sense of pride and 
ownership of the value individuals add to the service. It will guide the day-to-day operations, career planning and 
performance conversations of every public servant. Figure 5 below reflects the leadership statement for the public 
service of Jamaica. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 6: Leadership Statement for the Public Service of Jamaica 
 


----- PAGE 24 -----
 
 
23 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
The Core Values of the Public Service 
Values are the deeply ingrained principles that guide the Government’s actions; they serve as its cultural 
cornerstones. They drive how we interact, influence and work together to achieve the government’s priorities and 
objectives. Operating in accordance with the agreed public sector values can , therefore, strengthen the capacity of 
public sector organisations to operate more effectively, resulting in a high-performance environment with high levels 
of employee engagement. Conversely, a failure to adhere to these values can lead to poor performance, negatively 
impacting the quality of the service we provide to our citizens , and by extension, affecting the economic growth and 
the development of the country. 
Values in Relation to Competencies 
 
While competencies and values have significant areas of overlap, there are key differences between them: 
 Values are intangible. They are based on feelings, perceptions, preferences, and priorities —an internal 
code that influences how we experience and interpret the world. 
 Competencies are tangible. They define on -the-job behaviours that people around us can objectively 
observe and measure. 
 Values are important to the organisation, and competencies are the behaviours that support  
values. Whatever the value is, we identify the supporting competencies. It is a marriage between the ‘what’ 
and the ‘how’.  
Audience For Values In The Public Service 
 
Values apply to every public -sector employee , including executives, technical, administrat ive and auxiliary staff 
members. All employees are expected to embrace and model the agreed values . Permanent Secretaries and other 
heads of the MDAs have a responsibility to ensure that the values of the public service are promoted to and displayed 
by all public servants under their employment.  
 
 
 

----- PAGE 25 -----

Accountability Statement 
We operate according to established policies, principles,  codes of conduct and the standards of the Government of 
Jamaica. We open ourselves to scrutiny at every level, accept responsibility for the quality and timeliness of our 
deliverables in the most cost-effective manner, and take appropriate actions to ensure obligations are met. We take 
ownership of our workspace and consider each action and its effect on the citizenry, environment, and the larger 
economy. We have a sincere and dedicated focus on the success of our value for taxpayers’ money.

Structure of the Competency Framework 
Cluster Title 
The Core Competency Table Outline (see example in Figure 7) reflects the leadership cluste r for the specific 
competencies. Within each table is a cluster title.  
The cluster title highlights related competencies under a single high-level heading. The three cluster titles in this 
framework are: 
 Inspiring  
 Future-Oriented 
 Performance Driven 
Competency Title 
Each cluster consists of three competencies directly related to the leadership cluster. The nine competencies reflected 
across the three leadership clusters are:  
 Communicating effectively 
 Working collaboratively 
 Developing capability 
 Seeing the big picture 
 Driving continuous change and improvement 
 Making effective decisions 
 Demonstrating a commercial and business mindset 
 Ensuring a quality service 
 Ensuring Value for Tax Payers’ Money 
Competency Definition 
Each table carries a competency descriptor, which is a definition or statement of what the competency means.  
Sub -competencies  
Service-oriented 
Inclusivity 
Passion for Service 
Critical thinking 
 being team players who expect, value and reward collaborative 
behaviours and practices 

----- PAGE 30 -----
 
 
29 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Sub-competencies are core skills necessary to perform daily tasks. The sub -competencies are not exhaustive, but 
the selected ones are deemed most crucial for effective job performance. Other competencies will apply. 
Competency Indicators 
The competency indicators provide details of the competency at each proficiency level, providing examples of how 
the competency can be demonstrated. Notably, the indicators at each proficiency level are illustrative rather than 
definitive, which means that other similar examples of (knowledge, skills, and/or abilities) are also possible. Moreover, 
each proficiency level is cumulative, which means that indicators from lower levels will not be repeated at higher 
levels, although they still apply. The competency indicators define effective performance but do not provide specific 
“how to” indicators. For example, we do not tell you how to perform competencies such as conduct ing a learning 
needs analysis or gain ing buy-in from stakeholders, supervisors, or colleagues. Also, the competency indicators 
are not exhaustive documentation of the full spectrum of core competencies. Other indicators will apply.  
Ineffective Behaviours 
Ineffective behaviours are inadequate performances that , when continuously displayed , can inhibit the growth and 
development of the organisation. These descriptors of behaviour are not designed to be exhaustive but provide a 
sense of what is not expected nor acceptable from professionals in the public service o f Jamaica. Importantly, one 
example of ineffective behaviour is not necessarily an indication of an ineffective employee.  Therefore, the 
behaviours presented are mere examples of what public sector employees should avoid.  
Professional Bands 
Professional b ands indicate the expertise or mastery an individual has demonstrated in a competency. The 
Framework’s professional band scale ranges from band one to six, with illustrations. These illustrations provide 
examples of work behaviour at each professional leve l, reflecting increasing degrees of responsibilities and 
requirements. The band of sophistication and complexity of the competency gradually increases as the employee 
moves from one professional band to another. Also, the bands are accumulative, which means that each professional 
band assumes that the behaviours at the lower band have been mastered. The following table reflects the six 
professional bands illustrated in this framework.

Competency Profile  
The Inspiring Cluster 
Inspirational leadership is about motivating and energi sing oneself and others to strive towards excellent outcomes 
in job-related tasks. It is centred on demonstrating pride and passion for the public service, being inclusive in working 
with others , and being fair and impartial in the way we allocate resources and provide services to stakeholders. 
Inspirational leaders communicate their intentions clearly and listen to others so they can support and learn from 
them. Inspirational leaders learn from their own experiences and provide opportunities to share their learning and 
learn from each other. As inspirational leaders, we strive to make the GoJ where the best and the most talented want 
to work.

INEFFECTIVE BEHAVIOURS 
 Restricts collaboration in existing relationships and gives limited attention to the importance of building networks. 
 Creates and allows cliques and narrow ways of working within and across functions, promoting a ‘knowledge is power’ culture. 
 Gives team limited support on how to collaborate with delivery partners. 
 Neglects to create a wider network beyond immediate work group. 
 Struggles to address low morale or de-motivation within teams. 
 Does not engage in supporting the work of other teams or encouraging competition with other teams. 
 Displays little appreciation of the value of different contributions and perspectives. 
 Blames or is overly critical of people who have different working styles or development needs. 
 Misses opportunities to generate better outcomes for the customer through collaboration with others. 
 Does not support or enlist the support of others to get work done.

The Future-Oriented Cluster 
 
Future-oriented leadership is about anticipating the needs of the people and focusing energies on providing the 
direction needed to guide employees to achieve objectives. Future -oriented leaders are responsive and innovative 
and seek opportunities to create effective and sustainable change. As future -oriented leaders, we constantly look at 
the big picture instead of focusing on our part of the system only and understand how individual roles align with and 
support Government policy objectives and citizens’ needs.

Seeing the big picture

 Visioning 
 Horizon scanning 
 Systems thinking 
 Strategic thinking 
 Analytical thinking 
 Organisational awareness

INEFFECTIVE BEHAVIOURS 
 Focuses on short-term concerns, neglects long-term thinking about evolving and future issues and considerations for the Public Service and MDA. 
 Focuses on own immediate area of concern and does not see interconnections across Public – Service.

 Gives limited attention to the bigger issues and interactions across MDAs and outside the Public Service when defining strategy. 
 Lacks clarity of, or interest in, gaining wider stakeholder perspective. 
 Executes own tasks without considering how the work impacts or interacts with other teams. 
 Works on own tasks in isolation, showing little interest in the wider context and relevant developments outside immediate area.

INEFFECTIVE BEHAVIOURS 
 Retains ineffective structures, systems or programmes. 
 Acts impulsively. 
 Ignores developments in technology that could benefit Public Service delivery and policy implementation. 
 Supports incremental improvements within isolated areas rather than any fundamental shifts in approach. 
 Does not have a coherent approach to change management / is not able to articulate, design or implement a change management strategy which 
results in the change not having the desired effect on service delivery. 
 Creates a punitive environment for risk taking and responsive decision-making – shows intolerance of mistakes. 
 Spends limited time engaging experts and relevant individuals in developing and testing proposals, failing to pass on relevan t team member/s 
feedback. 
 Reluctant to consider ways to improve services in own area, even when improvements are urgently required. 
 Remains attached to out-dated procedures and technologies. 
 Fails to capture, utilise, and share customer insights in the development of policies and services. 
 Ignores the horizontal process flow of the MDA, resulting in things falling through the cracks and poor service to the customer.

Effective Decision-Making

risks, and arriving at solution that advances the strategic priorities of the Government.

 Data analysis and synthesis 
 Crisis management

The Performance Cluster 
Performance-driven leadership is about being responsive and striving to provide timely, high -quality services that make 
a difference in the lives of citizens in the most appropriate and cost -effective way. Performance -driven leaders h ave a 
business mindset and seek to provide the best value for every taxpayer dollar spent. As performance-driven leaders, we 
create and work to sustain a quality culture that considers the feedback of citizens and drive s continuous improvement 
and innovation. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 10: The Performance Cluster

INEFFECTIVE BEHAVIOURS 
 Allows non-core activities to divert funds and make decisions not in the best long-term financial interest of the MDA. 
 Pays little attention to accountability and regard for economy, efficiency, and effectiveness in the use of public funds and resources.

 Rarely uses financial or management information to support analysis and decision-making.

 Ignores the MDA’s financial procedures or breaks the rules for the sake of expediency. 
 Does not understand basic financial concepts. 
 Does not follow appropriate control or authorisation process for work, services or resources used.

INEFFECTIVE BEHAVIOURS

 Shows a lack of understanding of relevant commercial concepts and systems.

Providing a Quality Service

difference to the quality of life of the citizens of Jamaica.

 Knowledge of government and regulatory guidelines 
 Business knowledge 
 Interpersonal skills 
 Systems thinking 
 Knowledge of organisational metrics/analytics/business indicators

 Social awareness 
 Management of strategic and commercial partnerships 
 Data gathering and analysis

INEFFECTIVE BEHAVIOURS 
 Does not give sufficient attention to managing customer expectations at the highest level. 
 Fails to effectively capture, utilise and share customer insight appropriately in the development of policies and services. 
 Puts an overly strong focus on achieving tasks without efforts to motivate and energise people to perform. 
 Establishes unrealistic or confusing priorities and plans, leading to inefficiency in service overlaps between activities. 
 Takes little to no action when customer needs are not being met. 
 Makes changes to service delivery with minimal involvement of others. 
 Assigns work without support to develop the skills and knowledge needed to do the job.

 Tolerates poor service level from others/Does not encourage continuous improvement.

Professional Pathways and Technical Competencies  
Aligned to the Competency Framework is the professional pathways , which establishes core professional groups 
defined by standards of practice at each level of professional maturity. The professional pathways will, among other 
things, seek to: 
 elevate the level of professionalism and competence across critical technical areas in the public service 
through appropriate leadership, training and certification programmes 
 raise the formal status of professional groups across the public service 
  facilitate professional autonomy 
 allow for objectivity and transparency in Human Resource Managemen t and Development decisions , 
particularly those related to learning and development needs, promotion, recruitment, and selection 
 serve as a source of reference and impetus in sharpening the competencies of public sector employees 
and enhancing organisational capabilities to deal with existing and emerging challenges 
 assist academic institutions in aligning their curriculum to meet the industry's needs 
 ensure a steady supply of competent professionals in new growth areas 
 provide a platform for self -assessment to be aware of competency gaps, and engage in continuous 
learning to further careers and keep in tandem with the new demands.  
The professional pathways present a six-step process for public servants to move into and across professions in the 
service. I ndividuals would need to: 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 11: The Performance Cluster 
 


----- PAGE 64 -----
 
 
63 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
1. Identify their professional group  - Professional groups are broad categories of careers  that share similar 
characteristics and whose employment requirements c all for many common interests, strengths and 
competencies. 
2. Ascertain their proficiency level and professional band - The proficiency level and professional band indicate 
the level of expertise the individual is currently operating at in a specific job function (further explained in the 
core and technical competency outlines).  
3. Align educational requirements to ensure that the job level is commensurate with the academic qualification 
identified.  
4. Strengthen core and technical competencies needed to ensure optimal performance.  
5. Access learning pathways to become exposed to various learning opportunities to develop and update their 
knowledge skills and reflect on their learning experiences when gaps become evident or an individual 
plateaus in their career. The learning pathways is critical to the learning and development phase of the 
professional journey, which is ongoing from the onboarding of the public servant to the separation of same. 
Below are various learning pathways that public servants will be able to access throughout the life of their 
professional journey with the public sector of Jamaica. 
6. Determine the next stage of their career development - Having accessed learning pathways, an individual 
can advance in his/her career by way of promotion, further j ob enrichment or transitioning to another 
professional group where the experiences gained and the skills and competencies developed are relevant or 
transferrable.

Professional Development 
Professional development is a requirement for all employees to continually update their skills and knowledge to 
remain professionally competent. All public sector professionals must undertake and record/log online through the 
designated ICT platform a minimum of 30 hours of developmental activity each calendar year, which should include 
at least eight hours of formal or structured learning. Given that a wide range of activities can be qualified as 
professional development, these activities must be aligned to the learning pathways and approved by the head of the 
profession. 
What is a Profession? 
The term profession has multiple interpretations. Yet there are some common elements presented in an attempt to 
define the term. 
1. Undoubtedly, education standards and entrance into some professions are often set. So, the first element 
is specialised knowledge and expertise certified by a formal authority. All members of a profession are 
expected to have a certain degree of theoretically-based knowledge acquired through education or training 
at acknowledged institutions. 
2. A profession often has its own principles of conduct, value systems, work ethics and standards of 
behaviour with enforcement. That is, there are often demands on the practices of a profession described 
in a code of ethics. 

----- PAGE 66 -----
 
 
65 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
3. A profession can also be categorised as a sustained period of mentored experience or apprenticeship. 
In short, a profession is defined as an occupational field that requires formal qualification and training and has 
defined standards that guide the behaviours of the practitioners in said field.Technical Competencies 
Technical Competencies  
The Technical Competency outline is an inventory of critical k nowledge, skills, and/or abilities that lead to an 
acceptable performance level in specific jobs . It describes competencies in technical terms, using indicators to 
help recognise them when individually demonstrated at a particular proficiency level . The technical competency 
outline was developed to provide guidelines on the key functional/technical competencies necessary for effective  
performance for public servants in the various Ministries/Departments and Agencies in Jamaica. The technical 
competencies correlate with the knowledge, skills and abilities mapped across 17 professional groups through a 
thorough review of, among others, job descriptions, legislative documents, business plans, the Public Sector Learning 
Framework, the Modernisation Plan, the Vision 2030 and the National Development Plan, focus group discussions 
with technical experts in the specific job area, and research i nto international technical competency descriptors and 
levels for various technical functions.  
Objectives of the Technical Competency Framework 
The Technical Competency Framework is intended to, among other things: 
 Describe the skills, knowledge, and, in some instances, behaviours needed by professionals to be effective 
in their job function 
 Establish the proficiency standards required by professionals as they progress in their careers. 
 Serve as the foundation for the design and development of interventions that will improve the competencies 
of professionals in a particular technical field. 
 Provide guidance to aspiring professionals or professionals in other fields as to what is required to become 
accomplished professionals in a particular technical area. 
Guiding Principles 
The Technical Competency Framework was developed using several guiding principles. 
 Technical competencies must be generic enough to capture key processes within the particular professional 
group. Any professional within the professional group should be able to move across any MDA and function 
with the same degree or more of expertise at the level indicated. 

----- PAGE 67 -----
 
 
66 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
 Technical competencies should reflect the skills needed and level of proficiency expected of a professional 
within a particular technical role. 
 Technical competencies should be a basis for certification, education, and developmental programmes. 
 Technical competencies should guid e individuals within a particular technical area on how skills and 
proficiency needs change as their career progresses. 
Structure of the Technical Competency Outline 
The technical competency outline consists of core professional groups, technical competency indicators and 
proficiency levels. Technical competencies are typical competencies critical for success at a particular level in a 
professional group. They are not exhaustive and therefore will not preclude professionals within the group from 
developing other technical competencies.  
 
Core Professional Group : For all the technical competencies within this framework, there is a core professional 
group that details broad categories of careers sharing similar characteristics and whose employment requirements 
call for many common interests, strengths and competencies. There are 14 core professional groups identified t o 
form part of the professional pathways. 
  
Competency Descriptor: Each professional group then carries a competency descriptor, which is a definition or 
statement of what the technical competency means. There are several competencies across each professional group. 
The competencies are not exhaustive, but the selected ones are deemed most crucial for effective job performance 
within the professional group. Other technical competencies will apply. 
 
Competency Indicators:  The competency indicators provide de tails on specific technical aspects of the 
competency at each proficiency level, providing examples of how the competency can be demonstrated. Notably, the 
technical indicators at each proficiency level are illustrative rather than definitive, which means that similar examples 
of technical aspects (knowledge, skills, and/or abilities) are also possible. Moreover, each proficiency level is 
cumulative, which means that technical indicators from lower levels will not be repeated at higher levels but will still 
apply. The competency indicators define effective performance but are not meant to define how work should be 
performed in a particular technical area. Also, the competency indicators for the professional groups are not an 
exhaustive documentation of the full spectrum of technical competencies. Other indicators will apply.  
 
 
 

----- PAGE 68 -----
 
 
67 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Proficiency Levels: The technical competencies are demonstrated across four proficiency levels as follows: 
  Essential  
 Applied  
 Advanced  
 Expert 
It is expected that the foundational l evel which traditionally is required of entry -level employees will be developed 
through higher education engagements such as community colleges, sixth form, teacher training institutions , or 
HEART education and training , so employees will enter the service  already equipped with the basic knowledge to 
effectively carry out entry-level functions. This Framework, therefore, addresses Essential Skills and envisions a more 
capable, well-informed and sophisticated entry-level employee.   
 
The levels describe the degree of knowledge , skills or abilities the professional needs to have regardless of 
professional group or job function. Typical performance behaviours are associated with each proficiency level, and it 
is expected that each professional will also reflect the core competencies for each level indicated. There is, therefore, 
a close alignment between core band descriptors and those of technical; except for band six in the core competencies, 
which reflects professionals who are leaders of broad organisational  portfolios and not necessarily a particular 
technical function. The level of sophistication and complexity of the competency gradually increases as the employee 
moves from one proficiency level to another. The table below delineates the four proficiency l evels and the 
behavioural indicators for the technical competencies.

List of Professional Groups  
There are 17 professional groups in this Framework. The professional groups identified form the model of what 
technical competencies should look like in the creation of technical competencies across the Public Service in 
Jamaica. Professional groups such as Medical, Engineering , Education and Training that are widely known across 
the public service and have standard guidelines and established modes of operations. Active professional 
Associations that engage in set professional development interventions and have clear  procedures for mobility, 
transition and/or succession planning are not reflected in this framework. However, the standards set for the creation 
of technical competencies should be adopted by these professions and the Head of Profession is expected to develop 
and promulgate the framework accordingly. 
 
Other emerging professions such as Audit, Procurement and Information Communication Technolo gy have recently 
designed well-researched frameworks, but need to adopt the standard established by the Government of Jamaica 
Competency Framework and re-imagine what they have created accordingly to allow for clarity in expectations of 
behaviours.

Programme and Project Management 
  
Programme and Project Management include planning, organising, securing, controlling, leading and managing resources and tasks to achieve specific 
programme or project goals. Programme and Project professionals determine strategies to execute or implement programmes and projects, e valuate and 
understand project requirements, and analyse and gather the required human and other resources. Profession titles in this group include, but are not limited 
to, Project Manager, Project Officer, Consultant, Monitoring and Evaluation Officer and Quality Assurance Officer. The technical competencies for 
Programme and Project Management are related to the Project Management Life Cycle.

77 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Information Professionals-Records and Information Management  
 
According to the ISO 15489:2016 standard, Records and Information Management is the field of management responsible for the efficient and systematic 
control of the creation, receipt, maintenance, use and disposition of records, including the process of capturing and maintaining evidence of, and information 
about, business activities and transactions in the form of record s. Profession titles in this group include , but are not limited to , Archivist, Audio Visual 
Records Analyst, Campus Records Manager, Director Documentation Information and Access Services,  Information Manager, Records and Information 
Manager, Records Manag ement Analyst , Records Manager/Records Management Coordinator , Registrar, Records Officer, Records/ Registry Clerk , 
Records and Information Management Officer, Senior Records Officer, and Technical Information Manager.  
Technical Competencies Core Competencies Core Values 
 Records Maintenance and Use 
 Mail Correspondence 
Management

 Reference and Research 
Services 
 Retention and Disposition

Programmes

RIM Systems

95 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Human Resource Management and Development   
 
Human Resource Management and Development can be defined as the process of managing human resources and talents to achieve the entity’s objectives. 
This includes recruitment, selection, retention, compensation and benefits, employee and industrial relations, training and d evelopment, performance 
management and career development and also the management of employees’ health and safety in the organisation. 
Profession titles in this group include , but are not limited to , human resource personnel, Organisation Development Specialist, Compensation and Benefits 
Officer, Training and Development Practitioner, Industrial Relations Specialist, Occupational Health and Safety Officer, and Job Analyst Specialist. 
Technical Competencies Core Competencies Core Values 
 Workforce Planning and Talent 
Management 
 Employee Relations 
 Service Delivery and Information 
 Learning and Development 
 Organisation Development

 Demonstrating a Commercial and Business 
Mindset 
 Ensuring Value for Taxpayer’s Money 
 Providing Quality Service 
 Accountability 
 Inclusivity  
 Service Excellence  
 Integrity and Impartiality 
 Innovation

105 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Environmental Management 
 
Environmental management is concerned with the assessment and monitoring of changes in the natural and built environment and implementing measures 
to maximise human benefit and to minimise en vironmental degradation. Some activities include pollution monitoring and assessment, pollution prevention, 
air quality management, eco-systems management, protected areas management and environmental law and compliance. Profession titles include, but are 
not limited to, Environmental Manager/Specialist, Waste Management Officer, Environmental Analyst, Environmental Planner, Environmental Coordinator, 
Natural Resource Specialist, Sustainability Officer, Environmental Technician, and Enforcement Officer/Warden.  
Technical Competencies Core Competencies Core Values 
 Monitoring and Assessment 
 Planning 
 Ecosystems Management 
 Knowledge and Interpretation of 
Environmental and Planning 
Legislation 
 
 Communicating Effectively 
 Working Collaboratively  
 Developing Capability  
 Seeing the Big Picture  
 Driving Continuous Change and Improvements 
 Making Effective Decisions  
 Demonstrating a Commercial and Business 
Mindset 
 Ensuring Value for Taxpayer’s Money 
 Providing Quality Service 
 
 Accountability

113 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Office and Administrative Services  
 
Office and Administrative Support Services  is a broad category that includes tasks performed daily to ensure that the office is highly functional and 
effective. These tasks may include general office management such as inventory management, records management, managing inter nal and external 
communications, and assisting in ensuring the timely implementation of the strateg ic goals of the organisation. Profession titles in this group include , but 
are not limited to , Executive Secretary/Assistant, Administrative Secretary/Assistant, Mail Clerk, Postal Service Clerk, Telephone Operator/Switchboard 
Operator, Data Entry Clerk, Receptionist and Information Clerk, Messenger/Bearer, Inventory Clerk, Records Clerk and Records Administrator.

Facilities Management  
 
Facilities Management professionals are concerned with maintaining, improving and adapting the buildings and support services of an organisation to  
create a physical environment that supports the primary objectives of that organisation. Profession titles in this group include, but are not limited to, Facilities 
Manager, Property/Plant Officer/Manager/Coordinator, Office Manager, Maintenance Officer and Environmental Health and Safety Officer.

Financial Management and Accounting 
 

----- PAGE 127 -----
 
 
126 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Financial management and accounting professionals are engaged in the planning, directing, monitoring, organi sing, and controlling of the monetary 
resources of the organisation to ensure that it meets its strategic objectives. Profession titles in this group include, but are not limited to, Financial Officer, 
Risk and Insurance Officer, Checking Officer, Budget Officer, Financial Planner, Management Accountant, Financial Controller, Credit Manager, Treasurer, 
Cashier, Certifying Officer, Compliance Officer, Reconciliation Officer, Cash Manager and Salary Officer.

Management

Community and Social Service 
 
Community and Social Service professionals work in a variety of settings to assess needs  and plans, and coordinate activities for so cial interventions to 
create more empowered individuals and sustainable communities. The job demands vary but include duties such as community engagement and risk/needs 
assessments of clients to determine eligibility for social intervention under public so cial services and community development programmes.  Profession 
titles in this group include , but are not limited to , Counsellor, Psychologist, Probation Officer, Social Worker, Community Development Officer, Religious 
Worker, Health Educator, Social Work Aides, Welfare Case Managers and Guidance Counsellors.

Security and Protective Services 
 
The organised system of defensive measures is instituted and maintained at all levels of command with the aim of achieving and maintaining security. The 
security and protective services professionals are responsible for the p rotection of life and property, the preservation of peace and good order , and the 
prevention and detection of crime. Profession titles in this group include, but are not limited to, Correctional Officer, Police Officer, Security Guard and military 
personnel. 
Technical Competencies Core Competencies Core Values 
 Use of Force 
 Service Laws

 Providing Quality Service

 Inclusivity  
 Service Excellence  
 Integrity and Impartiality 
 Innovation

Strategic Planning 
 
Strategic planning  can be described as the systematic approach to clarifying  corporate objectives, supporting strategic decision -making and conducting 
performance monitoring and evaluation toward achieving the orga nisation’s vision. Profession titles in this group include , but are not limited to , Corporate 
Planner, Strategic Planner, Business Analyst, Performance Analyst, Researcher and Risk Analyst.  
 
Technical Competencies Core Competencies Core Values 
 Strategic Planning 
 Monitoring and Evaluation 
 Risk Analysis and Mitigation 
 Strategic Networking  
 
 Communicating Effectively 
 Working Collaboratively  
 Developing Capability  
 Seeing the Big Picture  
 Driving Continuous Change and Improvements 
 Making Effective Decisions  
 Demonstrating a Commercial and Business 
Mindset 
 Ensuring Value for Taxpayer’s Money 
 Providing Quality Service 
 
 Accountability

 Service Excellence  
 Integrity and Impartiality 
 Innovation

Policy Development & Analysis 
 
Policy professionals address public issues by engaging in the process of problem identification and assessment, formulation of solutions, and implementation 
of agreed initiatives through a consultative and advisory process. Profession titles include, but are not limited to, Policy Analyst and Policy Manager.

Legal Services 
 
Legal Services includ es the provision of legal advice, repre sentation, preparing legal opinions, reviewing and drafting legislative instruments and legal 
documents, managing litigation, negotiations, and supporting policy development for the efficient operation of the organisati on. Profession titles include , but 
are not limited to , General Counsel, Legal Officer, Legal Counsel, Case Attorney, Director of Legal Affairs, Crown Counsel, Legal Executive, and Clerk of 
Courts.

Government Communication 
 

----- PAGE 165 -----
 
 
164 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Government Communication refers to the process by which the Ministries, Departments and Agencies (MDAs) use strategic techniques, tools and technologies 
to build excellent relationships within the organisation and between th e organisation and its publics. It involves providing public education, building a good 
corporate image and effectively managing crises. Profession titles in this group include, but are not limited to, Public Education Manager, Information Manager, 
Promotion and Marketing Manager, Marketing Analyst, Public Relations Officer, Event Planner, Journalist, Communication Specialist, Social Media Officer, 
Public Affairs Advisor/Consultant/Manager, Editor, Writer, Multi-Media Artist and Animator, and Graphic Artist. 
Technical Competencies Core Competencies Core Values 
 Writing 
 Media Content Planning, Monitoring 
and  Analysis

170 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Audit  
 
According to the Definition of Internal Auditing in the IIA's International Professional Practices Framework (IPPF), internal auditing is an independent, 
objective assurance and consulting activity designed to add value and improve an organisation's operations. It helps an organisation accomplish its 
objectives by bringing  a  systematic,  disciplined approach to evaluating and improving the  effectiveness of risk management, control, and governance 
processes. Profession titles in this group include, but are not limited to, Internal Auditor, Auditor, Risk and Compliance Manager/Officer, Corporate or 
Business Auditor, and 
Compliance Investigator.

Procurement  
 
Procurement may be defined as the business management function of a Ministry, Department or Agency that ensures identification, sourcing, accessing 
and managing of the external resources that the MDA needs or may need to fulfill its strategic objectives. Profession titles in this group include, but are not 
limited to, Procurement Officer/Manager, and Director of Procurement. 
Technical Competencies Core Competencies Core Values 
 Regulatory Systems 
 Documentation Management 
 Contract Award 
 Contract Administration & 
Performance Management 
 Procurement Planning 
 Initiation and Management of 
Procurement 
 Communicating Effectively 
 Working Collaboratively  
 Developing Capability  
 Seeing the Big Picture  
 Driving Continuous Change and Improvements 
 Making Effective Decisions  
 Demonstrating a Commercial and Business 
Mindset 
 Ensuring Value for Taxpayer’s Money 
 Providing Quality Service 
 
 Accountability 
 Inclusivity  
 Service Excellence  
 Integrity and Impartiality 
 Innovation

Information and Communication Technologies (ICT) 
 
ICT is a field of work and study that in cludes technologies such as desktop and laptop computers, software, peripherals, and connections to the internet , 
which are intended to fulfill information processing and communications functions (UNESCO 2002, Statistics, Canada, 2008). Profession titles in this group 
include, but are not limited to , Chief Information Officer, Communications Trade Worker, Computer Network Professional, Database and Systems 
Administrator and ICT Security, ICT Business and Systems Analyst, ICT Manager, ICT Assistant, Web Developer, Programmer and Systems Analyst.

Service Experience 
 
Service Excellence can b e described as the intentional and systematic approach to ensuring that internal and external customers/clients have positive  
service experiences that allow them to meet their desired objectives. Service excellence professionals ensure that systems, p olicies and services are 
designed to meet the needs of the customer and the process benefits from their feedback. Profession titles in this group incl ude, but are not limited to , 
Customer Care Officer, Customer Service Monitoring and Evaluation Officer, Custome r Service Monitoring and Evaluation Manager, Customer Care 
Manager and Customer Service Director.   
Technical Competencies Core Competencies Core Values 
 Customer Care and Relationship 
Management  
 Customer Needs Assessment and 
Analysis  
 Service Design and Delivery 
 Service Monitoring and Evaluation  
 Service Excellence Strategies, 
Policies and Standards 
 
 Communicating Effectively 
 Working Collaboratively  
 Developing Capability  
 Seeing the Big Picture  
 Driving Continuous Change and Improvements 
 Making Effective Decisions  
 Demonstrating a Commercial and Business Mindset 
 Ensuring Value for Taxpayer’s Money 
 Providing Quality Service 
 
 Accountability 
 Inclusivity  
 Service Excellence  
 Integrity and Impartiality 
 Innovation
