Proposed Governance Framework for the Professional Pathways 
For the Professional Pathways to be embedded as part of how we do things in the GoJ , it is important to implement 
a robust framework to organi se and manage its implementation, operation and continued evaluation, change and 
development. All professions working across government will need to identify and/or implement their own governance 
structure based on stipulated standards and procedures. 
 
Definitions as used in the context of this proposed governance structure: 
 Profession - an occupational field that requires formal qualification and training and has defined 
standards that guide the behaviours of the practitioners in that field. 
 Governance – the arrangements put in place by members of each profession to guide the 
operations of that profession.  
 Regulation - Professional self-regulation ‘is a regulatory model that enables government to have 
some control over the practice of a profession and the services provided by its members without 
having to maintain in -depth expertise required to regulate a profession that would be required 
under direct regulation as provided in business or commerce.  
 
Some pr ofessions operating in GoJ have already adopted the regulations and standards of their international 
counterparts and developed their standards and practices , which are expected to continue. Emerging or newly 
established professions may wish to adopt the r egulations of counterpart professional bodies and even seek 
membership or affiliation where appropriate. 
All professions identified in the GoJ Professional Pathways shall be managed via the following: 
1. A Governing Council 
2. A Secretariat 
3. A Management Board 
4. Heads of Profession 
5. Cluster Managers 
6. Cluster Representatives 
 
 

----- PAGE 215 -----
 
 
214 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
The Governing Council  
The Governing Council (GC) shall be chaired by the Cabinet Secretary as Head of the Public Service and will consist 
of the following additional members: 
 Financial Secretary (MFPS) (Deputy Chair)  
 Chief Executive Officer (MIND)  
 Chief Personnel Officer of the Office of the Services Commission (OSC) 
 A representative from the Permanent Secretaries Board 
 President of the Jamaica Civil Service Association (JCSA) 
 Head of the Planning Institute of Jamaica (PIOJ) 
 
The Council shall set the strategic direction for continued development of the professions identified across the public 
sector, based on GoJ Priorities, Sustainable Development Goals, the National Development Plan, and the 
Modernisation and Transformation plans and programmes. It shall be the key decision -making body for whole -of-
government policy decisions for the development of public sector capability and capacity. The quorum for the Council 
shall be 5 members, including the Chair or Deputy Chair. 
 
The GC shall meet tri -annually (three times per year) at a minimum and will, at these meetings, address matters not 
limited to: 
 setting priorities for public sector learning and development 
 agreeing on any shifts in policies, standards and guidelines to secure ongoing public service capabilities to deliver 
on the Government’s priorities 
 leading the process for the development and approval by the Cabinet of a framework for the management of annual 
and supplementary public sector training and development budget  
 reviewing the annual return on investment (ROI) assessment for public sector learning and development and taking 
actions as necessary 
 monitoring the embedding of learning and development across the public service 
 monitoring the performance and effectiveness of the governance framework, with a formal review once every two 
years until otherwise determined 
 

----- PAGE 216 -----
 
 
215 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
The Secretariat 
The O ffice of the Cabinet (OoC) , in supporting the management of this policy, will provide the Secretariat t o the 
Governing Council (GC). The Secretariat will monitor the implementation of the Governing Council’s decisions. T his 
includes harmonising activities, coordinating and enabling the alignment of the Governing Council’s strategies and 
directives with the operations of the Management Board, the Heads of Professions and other stakeholders. 
The Management Board  
The GC shall delegate authority to the Management Board under the chairmanship of the Deputy Financial Secretary 
of the SHRMD to assist in the management of operational activities. SHRMD shall serve as secretariat to the Board.   
The Management Board shall comprise: 
 Cluster Representatives  
 Representative from MIND (Deputy Chair)  
 Representative from the Office of the Services Commission (OSC)  
 Representative from SHRMD  
 Representative from the Office of the Cabinet  
Two-thirds of the membership, including the Chair or Deputy Chair, shall constitute the quorum for the Board.  
The Management Board shall carry out the strategic directives of the GC and shall be responsible for: 
 ensuring competency requirements for each profession are correctly defined, aligned to government priorities 
and continue to meet the demands of the sector 
 advising on objectives for, and the direction of, professional groups for consideration by the Governing Council 
when setting strategy 
 determining the methodology to be used for the annual rotation of cluster representatives to comprise 
membership on the Board 
 identifying, analysing and addressing emerging issues (strategic and op erational) associated with 
Professional Groups and escalating to the GC those issues that require a policy decision 
 defining and assessing the performance of professions 
 
 

----- PAGE 217 -----
 
 
216 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
The Heads of Professions 
Each professional group shall have a Head of Profession. A HoP is recognised as an expert in the discipline and has 
MDA-wide impact and influence. This role shall be clearly articulated in the job descriptions (see Appendix 3 for a list 
of positions) that carry this parallel responsibility.  
Heads of Professions shall:  
 provide leadership of the profession/technical function 
 promote the integration of knowledge of technical functions across the MDA 
 influence policy and provide policy advice in line with the technical areas  
 create and lead professional networks, associations and engagements locally and internationally 
 work with the professional leads in MDAs and Cluster Managers to ensure the profession is well placed to 
support delivery of Government’s priorities 
 build partnerships within the professional group and across other professional groups within and outside the 
cluster 
Cluster Managers 
Cluster management shall be a function within MIND, with each manager assigned to a cluster of professions, 
facilitating the day -to-day operations of the professional groups w ithin that cluster and assuring maintenance of the 
competency standards and compliance with all policies and procedures within the talent management lifecycle. Cluster 
Managers shall, therefore: 
 support Heads of Profession s through monitoring and periodic reporting on learning, development, and 
networking within and across professions 
 liaise with Heads of Profession s as necessary , in the preparation and implementation of Learning and 
Development activities 
 support the coordination and management of professi onal networks, associations and engagements locally 
and internationally 
 recommend new initiatives to support the development of professional groups to meet the Government’s 
priorities 
 set and pursue targets, goals and objectives to achieve successful outcomes for professional groups 
 ensure that development interventions and learning products comply with established standards 
  support the collection and maintenance of records on professional groups and their activities in accordance 
with GoJ’s Records Management Policy 

----- PAGE 218 -----
 
 
217 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
 support the coordination and delivery of learning and development services within and among professional 
groups to increase public service effectiveness, efficiency and impact 
 foster teamwork  and a harmonious learning environment , and promote working collaboratively within and 
across professional groups 
 prepare quarterly and other reports on the activities/progress of the professional groups as may be requested 
 Identify and evaluate risks and opportunities associated with the development of the professional groups and 
take appropriate action 
 monitor the implementation of learning and development activities in accordance with the approved capacity 
and capability development plan and the established monitoring and evaluation framework 
Cluster Representatives 
The Professional Groups shall be organised according to clusters, each cluster represented on the Management Board 
by a designated Head of Profession.  
The role of a Cluster Representative shall rotate among the Heads of Profession within a clus ter according to a 
methodology to be agreed upon by the Management Board.  
A cluster representative (CR) will be nominated from among the Heads of Profession for each cluster and shall:    
• be responsible for representing the interests of the Cluster at the Management Board 
• convene meetings of the Heads of Profession at least once per quarter to: 
o identify, analyse and address emerging issues (strategic and operational) associated with Professional 
Groups and escalate to the MB those issues that require a policy decision/redress  
o monitor and evaluate the processes that are in place to identify and analyse cluster needs; 
o develop strong networks across the cluster and with other professional clusters 
o identify and engage key partners to support the development of the cluster 
o monitor and report on the activities of the cluster 
o assess the performance of professions within the cluster 
The office of the relevant Cluster Manager at MIND shall serve as the Secretariat for the cluster meetings.

Movements within and across Professional Groups 
 
If an employee wishes to move to another professional group or attain an alternative designation, they must meet the 
competency, educational and training requirements as set by the Competency framework and be approved upon 
evaluation by the professional group's board or governing council. Approval by the profession’s board does not negate 
other public sector requirements , such as the competitive  interview process. However, the level of competence 
demanded by that professional group’s board should be critical to the interview process. The Profession Board should 
consist of a group of individuals who provide top-level strategy for the continuing advancement and professionalisation 
of the profession. They should work with the Office of the Services Commission, Strategic Human Resource 
Management and Development and the Cabinet Office to ensure continuous professional development  and 
adherence to educational requirements and competency standards.  
 
Code of Conduct  
A code of conduct should be developed for each profession. There are two broad types of code s: aspirational and 
enforceable. Aspirational codes aim to inspire and guide those who want to do a professional job. Enforceable codes 
aim to reassure the profession al, employers, clients , and even the general public that all in the profession are 
operating to the standards expected. It is anticipated that the professions will develop enforceable codes of conduct. 
Defining each profession:  
In taking forward the work of each profession, the governance will ensure that the profession is fully defined. This 
includes defining: 
 its purpose 
 unique and identifying characteristics 
 any specific criteria for membership of the profession 
Roles to be played: 
 Head of the Public Service  
 Heads of Public Service Professions (HOPSPs) 
 Heads of Professions (HOPs)  
 Strategic Human Resource Development (SHRMD)  
 

----- PAGE 221 -----
 
 
220 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Appointments 
The Head of the Public Service will appoint one  person to be the Head of Public Service Professions, agreed upon 
as part of the professional pathway s project. This person who will also be appoint ed Chief Personnel Officer (CPO) 
will be known as the Head of Public Service Professions (HoPSPs). 
The Head of the Public Service will appoint one person to lead each of the professions  under the professional 
pathways project. The appointed person will be highly respected in the profession as a leader of professional 
excellence and will be able to command the su pport of members. These per sons will be known as Heads of 
Professions (HOPs). 
Membership of a Profession 
Any member of the Public Service may be accredited to any profession as long as they meet the criteria established 
by the Professional Pathways. 
 
Suppo rting Foundations of the Governance Framework  
The Governance Framework is underpinned by five supporting strands that support continuous development and 
consistency. 
 Good Leadership and Leadership Roles 
 Standards and Competences 
 Qualifications and Learning Curriculum 
 Talent Management  
 Networks

Appointment  
The Head of Public Service Professions (HoPSP s), who will also be the Chief Personnel Officer (CPO) , will be 
appointed by the Governor General who will issue the appropriate letter of appointment to the appointee in keeping 
with the provisions set out by Parliament. 
Role  
Provide leadership to all Professions: Good leadership is key to the success of the Public Service. Leading the 
newly created professions across Government Ministries Departments and Agencies has even more leadership 
challenges. This role will be the link to the Head of the Public Service  (HoPS) and will coordinate the work of the 
Heads of Profession s. The Head of Public Service Profession (HoPSP) is responsible for ensuring that the  
components below are in place or must drive the action to implement and embed them. 
 
The Role of Head of Profession (HoPs) in Embedding Professions in the Public Sector 
Being visible and engaged 
 
 Knowing the extent and depth of the professional communities  
 Acting as a champion/advocate and promoter of professions 
 Communicating the vision for the professions within and outside the 
Public Service 
 Engaging Heads of Profession to ensure a coordinated approach 
across the Public Service 
Supporting business delivery  
 
 Setting the objectives of the professions to support the Government’s 
service delivery agenda 
 Working with Heads of Profession and business leaders to ensure the 
profession is well placed to support delivery of Government’s priorities 
 
Building partnerships  
 
 Working with the Head of the Public Service, Heads of MDAs and 
Strategic HR, appointing Heads of each Profession 
 Building cross-government professional networks 
 Building links with the private  and voluntary sector and professional 
bodies as appropriate 
Building professional skills  
 
 Articulating a strategy to build professional skills to bridge any current 
and future capability gaps 
Championing learning and 
development (L&D) 
 Promoting L&D priorities for professionals 
 Advising on the balance between core and professional skills 
requirements 
Acting as a role model for L&D 
and Continuing Professional 
Development (CPD)  
 
 Cultivating and encouraging talent management 
 Providing professional assurance at recruitment, selection  and  
performance discussions for key roles 
 Identifying key professional posts across the Public  Service and 
developing appropriate strategies for succession planning 
 Coordinating development campaigns to attract and retain talent

Standards and Competencies of the Profession 
 Professional standards and competencies must be driven by the needs of the business. For certain 
professions, they may also be dictated by external governing bodies and qualifications.  
 
 Standards and competencies act as the cornerstone of the profession in its recruitment, performance 
management and learning and development activities, and as such , reinforce consistency and cohesion 
across the Public Service.  
 
 Professional bodies use standard s and competenc ies to define their particular profession and the skills, 
knowledge and behaviours professionals should be able to demonstrate. They are used for recruitment, 
training, Continuous Professional Development (CPD), appraisal and capability planning.  
 
 Standards, including codes of conduct, set out aspects of professional responsibility and behaviour and are 
important in shaping the expectations and behaviours of a profession’s membership. They may also set out 
how the profession wishes to be perceived by the public, customers, networks and stakeholders.  
 
 Most regulatory or professional bodies have existing detailed code of conduct, professional standards, and 
expected behaviours framework.  
 
 Competency Frameworks articulate the capability requirements of a profession, covering all relevant areas 
of professionalism. Although a Competency Framework for a profession may be separate from the generic 
or core competency framework of the organisation, both frameworks should complement each other.  

----- PAGE 229 -----
 
 
228 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
 
 Professional Competency Frameworks should be accompanied by clear guidance on how they should be 
utilised alongside generic frameworks. A professional competency framework for all GoJ professions has 
been developed to complement the creation of the professions . It will eventually be available o n MDA 
websites and on MyHR+. 

----- PAGE 230 -----
 
 
229 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Qualifications and Learning Curriculum  
It is best practice for the profession to set out appropriate and recommended learning and development activities 
as a professional curriculum, which may include qualifications. The scope of the curriculum will be determined by 
the profession’s needs. The curriculum should be structured, setting out learning options for appropriate levels 
within the profession. It should be regularly reviewed to ensure  learning continues to meet the needs of the 
business. 
In the context of the professions, 
qualifications serve two important 
purposes:  
 
1. Accredit members by requiring specified standards of education, 
training and experience, thus enabling an individual to be 
certified as a professional in their field.  
 
2. Validate members. Professionals can point to their status as 
proof of their skill and experience. Some professions may require 
a qualification for entry to the profession or qualifications at 
certain levels for specific posts, roles or grades. The routes to 
qualification are becoming more diverse with the development of 
pathways increasingly based on accumulated experience and 
practice. Where qualifications are deemed necessary, they 
should be accredited by a recognised awarding body.  
 
In the context of the professions, a 
curriculum may include: 
 
1. Specialist learning, such as profession -specific courses, externally 
accredited programmes and C ontinuing Professional Development 
activities.  
2. External activities, such as work placements.  
3. Generic learning, such as leadership and management and core 
skills programmes.  
4. Recommended on-the-job learning activities, such as shadowing or 
project work. 
5. Learning resources, such as case studies, pocketbooks, guides, etc. 
6. Further L&D options will eventually be available on MyHR+. 
 

----- PAGE 231 -----
 
 
230 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
 
Talent Management  
It is vital to develop and retain skilled staff to enhance organisational and professional capability. The profession 
must work across departmental boundaries to identify an d open up career development opportunities for its 
members. 
Identifying capability  
 
Initially, the profession will need to understand current capability , any 
skills deficits, and where capability will need to be developed for the 
future.  
Career pathways  
 
 Professions need a strategy that promotes accessible career 
pathways and development opportunities that meet the 
organisation’s requirements and benefits the individual.  
 The profession should be able to identify career pathways at all levels 
that offer both vertical progression and lateral development.  
 Career pathways may include the opportunity to gain recognised 
qualification. 
 Equally important should be the opportunity to acquire experience 
and knowledge from secondments, interchanges, job swaps/rotation, 
project work and working for short periods in the private sector.  
 
Strategic deployment  
The profession should have a structured deployment programme  to facilitate the movement of staff into career -
enhancing posts as required by the business. Suc h postings will create the opportunity to stretch and challenge 
talented individuals. To be fully effective, this is likely to require cross-organisation collaboration. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

----- PAGE 232 -----
 
 
231 The  Government of Jamaica’s Competency Model - Core and Technical Competency Outline  
 
Networks  
Networks promote collaboration and consistency internally and externally to the organisation. They assist in sharing 
best practices, generating discussion, debating, identifying emerging issues, promoting consensus and informing 
future strategies. A strong, proactive network may help a profession raise its profile, h ave a voice across the 
organisation, exert appropriate influence, and promote the profession’s value to the business. Professional 
networks can operate at three key levels: 
Penetrating networks working across 
professions and departments. This 
provides for:  
 
 
 Improved communication  
 Involved membership  
 Raised profile and influence  
 Inter-profession collaboration  
 Inter-MDA collaboration  
 Joined-up organisation  
 Sharing of best practices 
 Promotes interchange and transferable skills 
Working relationship with the 
professional or regulatory body. This 
provides for:  
 
 
 Professional strategy and oversight  
 Code of Conduct and ethical practice  
 Profession champion and representation  
 Governance  
 Extended professional networks  
 Conferences and seminars  
 Career planning and advice  
 Specialist and Expert  
 Education and training  
 Benchmark qualifications  
 Information and guidance  
 Consultancy 
External partnerships with the private 
& other sectors. This provides for:  
 
 Promoting the profession  
 Enhancing the ‘big picture’ view  
 Supporting learning and development  
 Assisting with skills and knowledge transfer  
 Supporting interchange and career pathways

Networks  
 The importance of these networks will vary across professions. A t a 
minimum, the profession should be working to overcome silo-working 
and barriers and promoting inter -profession and cross -organisation 
collaboration.
